Define a threshold time instead by
\[
t_x(\delta)=\inf\{t\geq0:D_x(t)\leq\delta\}.
\]
A time saving of at least $5\%$ means $t_h(\delta)\leq0.95t_c(\delta)$.
Here $\delta$ is a distance threshold, not a temperature.

\paragraph{Monotonicity makes threshold roots usable.}
For the irreducible reversible dynamics studied in the physical section,
KL decreases strictly along a nonstationary trajectory. A threshold between
its initial value and zero has a unique crossing time. If a candidate time
$s$ satisfies $D_h(s)\leq\delta$, then $t_h(\delta)\leq s$.
Thus, taking $s=0.95t_c(\delta)$, a sufficient condition for the desired
time saving is
\[
D_h(0.95t_c(\delta))\leq D_c(t_c(\delta))=\delta.
\]
The equation explains the logic; the certificate must still handle the
unknown cold root and every allowed parameter vector.

\paragraph{Normalize time carefully.}
The physical study writes $u=t/\tau$, where
$\tau=-1/\lambda_2$ is the relaxation time of the chosen generator.
For a fixed parameter vector, both preparations share that same $\tau$,
so a fractional target-time comparison is unchanged by normalization.
Across a parameter box, however, $\tau$ itself changes. A dimensionless
window does not automatically specify one shared number of seconds for
all models. An attempt frequency is needed to turn the clock into seconds.

\paragraph{The certified physical-model benchmarks.}
The retained study establishes $D_h\leq0.9D_c$ and $D_c\geq10^{-3}$ for
$0.4\leq u\leq0.8$, and $t_h(\delta)\leq0.95t_c(\delta)$ for every
$\delta\in[10^{-3},10^{-2}]$, throughout its specified simultaneous
$\pm5\%$ parameter box. The perturbed coordinates are three energy gaps,
three shared barrier excesses, and two temperature excesses $T_x/T_b-1$.
The last phrase matters: perturbing a temperature excess is not identical
to perturbing the full temperature ratio. The eight continuous obligations
and their reductions are recorded in Part II.

\clearpage
\section*{Lesson 29. What the physical model adds, and what remains idealized}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{29. What the physical model adds}
The exact counterexample was designed to settle a mathematical inference.
The physical-model study asks a different question using a constrained
thermal network. Its rate law has Arrhenius form
\[
a_{ij}=\nu_0\exp[-\beta_b(B_{ij}-E_j)],\qquad B_{ij}=B_{ji}.
\]
Here $\nu_0$ is an attempt frequency and $B_{ij}$ is a shared saddle energy.
The exponent uses the departure energy $E_j$, consistent with the
 destination-first index convention. A higher barrier above the departure
state suppresses that transition.

\paragraph{Detailed balance follows from the symmetric saddle.}
Multiply the rate by the unnormalized bath weight $q_j=e^{-\beta_b E_j}$:
\[
a_{ij}q_j
=\nu_0e^{-\beta_b(B_{ij}-E_j)}e^{-\beta_b E_j}
=\nu_0e^{-\beta_b B_{ij}}
=a_{ji}q_i.
\]
The departure energy cancels. This is a transparent physical construction
of reversible rates, rather than a separate balance constraint fitted
after the fact.

\paragraph{Shared parameters reduce arbitrary freedom.}
The study uses four ordered energies with three gaps and two interleaved
basins, $\{1,4\}$ and $\{2,3\}$. The saddle is
$B_{ij}=\max(E_i,E_j)+h_{ij}$, with one shared excess for each intrabasin
edge type and one for all interbasin edges. The six edges therefore do not
have six independently fitted barrier excesses. This geometry makes the
model more constrained and supplies a kinetic interpretation of the
collective relaxation.

\paragraph{Do not merge the two research claims.}
Two triplets in this physical example satisfy the original triplet test.
It is therefore not another witness to the all-triplet-failure conjecture.
The abstract counterexample and the robust physical example answer
different questions. Keeping them separate prevents a compelling finite-time
plot from being used as evidence for a premise it does not satisfy.

\paragraph{A realization is not an experiment.}
Part II gives an energy-conserving stochastic collision realization of the
rates and examines an explicit activated-intermediate construction. These
make assumptions more inspectable. They do not identify a particular
material, prove that a laboratory contact process obeys the idealization,
or include all costs of preparing a hotter initial state. A demonstrated
reduction in the model's relaxation time is not automatically a net energy
or engineering advantage for a device. That latter question requires an
account of preparation, coupling, measurement, and operating constraints.

\paragraph{A productive next layer of work.}
To connect the model to an experiment, specify what each state represents,
how rates are measured, how the bath approximation is maintained, and which
observable supplies the stopping criterion. Then compare model predictions
with measured uncertainty. The mathematical certificate is valuable because
it makes one link in that chain explicit; it does not claim the other links
have already been established.

\clearpage
\section*{Lesson 30. What a formal proof checks, and how to read its scope}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{30. Formal proof and reproducibility}
A proof assistant checks a mathematical argument written in a formal
language against explicit definitions and inference rules. The central
question becomes: what exact proposition was checked? A successful build
of one theorem does not imply that every sentence in a surrounding report
has been formalized.

\paragraph{Start with the definitions.}
For this project, a formal counterexample must define the actual generator,
its bath weights, the preparation distributions, the triplet predicate,
and the chosen distance. If any of these definitions differs from the
informal claim, a formally correct theorem could still answer the wrong
question. Source-to-statement correspondence is therefore a separate
review obligation. The same applies to exact computational certificates.

\paragraph{The covered results.}
The Lean companion verifies the rational-mode counterexample, an
initial-to-eventual Pearson chi-square reversal under its master equation,
and the sharp four-state amplitude supremum. It uses a particular sharpness
family. The report's stronger all-strict, uniformly gapped family has a
separate analytic and computational justification; it is not made formal
merely because a related family is formalized. The physical interval
certificates are also a distinct verification route.

\paragraph{What reproducibility should expose.}
A useful reproduction record identifies the inputs, source version, command,
output, and interpretation. A cryptographic hash is a compact identifier
of file contents. Matching hashes tie an old result to the same bytes;
they do not prove those bytes implement the intended mathematics. A changed
source invalidates that simple correspondence until the change has been
reviewed. This is why the accompanying guide tracks source-bound proof
reviews separately from passing replay logs.

\paragraph{A practical reading order for a theorem.}
First read its hypotheses, including positivity, simplicity, temperature
range, and chosen distance. Then read the conclusion literally: existence,
all-time ordering, eventual ordering, or a quantitative bound. Next identify
which definitions give those words mathematical content. Finally inspect
the proof and its dependencies. Running the command comes after knowing
what a successful command is meant to establish.

\paragraph{How much confidence is justified?}
Independent exact arithmetic, alternate spectral arguments, negative
controls, interval bounds, and formal checking each address different
failure modes. Their agreement is useful evidence. None should be
represented as external peer review or experimental confirmation. The
report preserves those distinctions so that another researcher can decide
what to reuse and what to check independently.

\clearpage
\section*{Lesson 31. Exercises with complete worked answers}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{31. Exercises with complete worked answers}
These problems revisit the dependency chain. Try each calculation before
reading its answer. Their purpose is to reveal whether a symbol has acquired
a concrete meaning, not to test speed.

\paragraph{Exercise 1: normalize and compare.}
Given weights $(1,1/2,1/8)$, find the probabilities and the ratio of the
third to the first. \emph{Answer:} the sum is $13/8$, so the probabilities
are $(8/13,4/13,1/13)$. Their ratio is $(1/13)/(8/13)=1/8$, equal to the
ratio of the original weights. Normalization changes entries but cancels
from pair ratios.

\paragraph{Exercise 2: translate a temperature ordering.}
If $T_h=4T_b$ and $T_c=2T_b$, find the inverse temperatures in bath units.
\emph{Answer:} $\beta_h=1/4$, $\beta_c=1/2$, and $\beta_b=1$.
Both preparations lie on the cooling side, with $0<1/4<1/2<1$.
The preparation called ``cold'' is still hotter than the bath.

\paragraph{Exercise 3: construct both directions.}
Let $q=(1,1/4)$ and choose downward rate $a_{12}=2$. Find the upward rate,
generator, equilibrium, and nonzero eigenvalue. \emph{Answer:}
$a_{21}=2(1/4)=1/2$, so
\[
M=\begin{pmatrix}-1/2&2\\1/2&-2\end{pmatrix},
\qquad \pi_b=(4/5,1/5)^\top.
\]
The columns sum to zero, and the equilibrium flows are
$2(1/5)=(1/2)(4/5)=2/5$. One eigenvalue is zero; their sum is the trace
$-1/2-2=-5/2$, so the other is $-5/2$. Direct multiplication by $(1,-1)^\top$
checks the nonzero mode independently.

\paragraph{Exercise 4: verify a covariance sign.}
Use $p=(1/2,1/2)$, $E=(0,2)$, and $w=(1,-1)$.
\emph{Answer:} $\langle E\rangle=1$, $\langle w\rangle=0$, and
$\langle wE\rangle=-1$, so $\Cov(w,E)=-1$. For a fixed observable in a
Gibbs family passing through these probabilities, $b'=1$ there. The
pairwise form gives $(1/2)(1/2)(-2)(2)=-1$ as a second check.

\paragraph{Exercise 5: distinguish sign and magnitude.}
Suppose certified intervals give $b_h\in[-0.21,-0.20]$ and
$b_c\in[-0.24,-0.23]$. Prove a strict magnitude advantage and bound its
fraction conservatively. \emph{Answer:} $|b_h|\leq0.21<0.23\leq|b_c|$.
Thus $1-|b_h|/|b_c|\geq1-0.21/0.23=2/23$, about $8.7\%$.
Using interval midpoints would give an estimate, not this guaranteed bound.

\paragraph{Exercise 6: calculate the two-exponential crossing.}
For $f_h=e^{-t}+9e^{-10t}$ and $f_c=2e^{-t}+e^{-10t}$, determine their
ordering. \emph{Answer:} $f_h-f_c=e^{-10t}(8-e^{9t})$.
The prefactor is positive, so the difference is positive before
$t=\log8/9$, zero there, and negative after it. This is a complete sign
argument, with no plotting or root-finding tolerance.

\paragraph{Exercise 7: separate amplitude, divergence, and time.}
If a one-mode amplitude ratio is $0.8$, what is the late-time KL ratio?
\emph{Answer:} $0.8^2=0.64$, giving a $36\%$ asymptotic KL reduction.
With slow decay rate $r$, the leading target-time advance is
$\log(1/0.8)/r$. Its percentage depends on the reference target time.
No universal percentage time saving follows from the amplitude ratio alone.

\paragraph{Exercise 8: find the missing logical check.}
A program finds a four-state reversal, but checks only three of the four
triplets. Does it certify the counterexample? \emph{Answer:} no. The
antecedent requires all four predicates to fail. A single omitted triplet
could satisfy the test and invalidate the proposed contradiction even if
the reversal itself is real.

\paragraph{Exercise 9: understand a strict supremum.}
Every allowed model has $A<1/4$, while a sequence of allowed models has
$A_m\to1/4$. Can one claim an allowed model attains $25\%$?
\emph{Answer:} no. The two facts establish the supremum exactly, but the
strict bound rules out attainment. A limit outside the allowed domain
does not supply a missing maximizer.

\paragraph{Exercise 10: identify insufficient robustness evidence.}
A numerical study samples a parameter box, finds no violations, and reports
a $5\%$ guarantee everywhere. What is missing? \emph{Answer:} a proof
covering every point between samples and numerical error in the evaluated
quantities. A complete interval cover can provide such a proof if every
subbox is successfully certified and the implementation bounds are valid.
Random sample count alone cannot provide it.


\paragraph{Exercise 11: verify a prefix contrast.}
Use normalized weights $q=(4/10,3/10,2/10,1/10)$.
Find $h^{(2)}$ and its squared weighted length.
\emph{Answer:} $Q_2=7/10$ and $Q_2/q_3=7/2$, so
$h^{(2)}=(1,1,-7/2,0)$. Its weighted mean is
$4/10+3/10-(2/10)(7/2)=0$. Its squared length is
$4/10+3/10+(2/10)(49/4)=63/20$. A signed contrast can have length larger
than one; it is not a probability vector.

\paragraph{Exercise 12: test a Schur elimination by substitution.}
Suppose an eigenvalue equation has $e=-5$, $\lambda=-2$,
$c=(1,2)^\top$, and $d=(3,4)^\top$. Find the correction to $A$.
\emph{Answer:} $\delta=3$ and
\[
\frac{cd^\top}{\delta}
=\frac13\begin{pmatrix}3&4\\6&8\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The bottom equation gives $z=(3x_1+4x_2)/3$.
Substituting $cz$ into the top equation yields exactly this correction.
This example checks the elimination algebra; no claim is made that an
arbitrary choice of $A$ gives a Markov generator or the proposed eigenvalue.

\paragraph{Exercise 13: detect an illegal division.}
An interval algorithm encloses a denominator by $[-0.01,0.02]$ and then
reports the quotient using the reciprocals of the endpoints.
\emph{Answer:} the step is invalid because the interval includes zero.
The reciprocal is unbounded near zero. One must establish a narrower
nonzero enclosure, split and analyze the domain with the singularity
handled, or reformulate the comparison without that division. A small
interval is not enough; its position relative to zero matters.

\paragraph{Exercise 14: turn a distance bound into a time bound.}
Assume a strictly decreasing $D_h(t)$, and let $D_c(t_c)=\delta$.
If $D_h(0.95t_c)\leq\delta$, show the time saving.
\emph{Answer:} by time $0.95t_c$ the hot trajectory has already reached
the threshold. Its first crossing therefore satisfies
$t_h\leq0.95t_c$. Subtracting from $t_c$ gives
$t_c-t_h\geq0.05t_c$, a saving of at least $5\%$ relative to $t_c$.
A pointwise comparison $D_h(t_c)<D_c(t_c)$ alone would establish only
$t_h<t_c$, without the quantitative $5\%$ margin.

\clearpage
\section*{Lesson 32. Map the foundations into the advanced report}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{32. Map the foundations into the advanced report}
The next part preserves the technical research exposition. It uses the
language now developed, but it is intentionally denser. Use the following
routes to move between explanation and proof rather than trying to absorb
every theorem in one uninterrupted reading.

\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.65\textwidth}}
\toprule Question & Route through the report\\\midrule
\endhead
What exactly was contradicted? & Read the source predicate and
Section~\ref{sec:claimscope}; match each hypothesis to its witness before
reading the eigenvalue calculation. Revisit Lessons 1, 19, and 20.\\[5pt]
Why does the overlap imply relaxation reversal? & Read
Section~\ref{sec:cooling} with Lessons 14, 16, and 17. Track the remainder
and the simple-slow-mode assumption.\\[5pt]
Can I check the original witness independently? & Read
Theorem~\ref{thm:counterexample}, the cofactor appendix, and
Section~\ref{sec:separatecheck}. Reconstruct the rates as in Lesson 18 and
understand the enclosures as in Lesson 21.\\[5pt]
What creates the effect? & Read Section~\ref{sec:mechanism}, then the
contrast argument in Section~\ref{sec:orderedconductances}. Revisit the
covariance identity and prefix contrasts.\\[5pt]
Why is the amplitude bound exactly one quarter? & Read
Section~\ref{sec:fulltripletbound} after Lesson 24. Separate the structural
lemma, the conditional-average bound, and the construction proving
sharpness. They are three different proof obligations.\\[5pt]
What changes without the triplet premise? & Read
Section~\ref{sec:universal} for the four-state classification and
Section~\ref{sec:degenerateextension} for repeated slow spaces.
Lessons 25--26 explain the geometry and the invariant projection.\\[5pt]
How do exact algorithms decide a case? & Read
Section~\ref{sec:exactclassifier} and the later rational-input classifier.
Watch assumptions on energies, exact root isolation, equality handling,
and failure reporting.\\[5pt]
What survives at finite time? & Read Section~\ref{sec:finitechi} for a
specified pair and the finite-time order sections for their precise
hypotheses. Eventual order alone is not their proof.\\[5pt]
What is physically demonstrated by the model? & Read
Sections~\ref{sec:physicalresults}, \ref{sec:physicalboxcertificate}, and
\ref{sec:thermalcollision}. Keep the two experiments of Lessons 18 and
29 separate.\\[5pt]
What has been formalized? & Read Section~\ref{sec:lean} with Lesson 30.
Match named theorems to the stated definitions and scope.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\paragraph{A compact symbol dictionary.}
\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.68\textwidth}}
\toprule Symbol & Meaning and common trap\\\midrule
\endhead
$i,j,k,n$ & State indices and number of states; an index is a label, not a time.\\
$E_i$ & Energy of state $i$; only energy differences affect Gibbs probabilities.\\
$T,\beta$ & Temperature and inverse temperature; their orderings are opposite.\\
$\beta_b,\pi_b$ & Fixed bath inverse temperature and its Gibbs distribution.\\
$q_i,Q$ & Positive bath weights and their sum; some advanced sections explicitly
use normalized $q=\pi_b$ instead. Read the local definition.\\
$\pi(\beta),Z(\beta)$ & Gibbs preparation and its partition function.\\
$p(t)$ & Evolving probability vector; generally not itself a Gibbs preparation.\\
$a_{ij},M_{ij}$ & Off-diagonal rate from $j$ to $i$; columns, not rows,
conserve probability in this convention.\\
$M,L$ & Probability generator and observable generator $L=M^\top$.\\
$D,S$ & Diagonal bath-probability matrix and symmetric transform
$S=D^{-1/2}MD^{1/2}$.\\
$\lambda_2,v_2,w_2$ & Slow nonzero eigenvalue, right eigenvector, and left
eigenfunction; simplicity must be checked.\\
$b(\beta)$ & Slow overlap $w_2^\top\pi(\beta)$, with a fixed normalization.\\
$\Cov,\Var$ & Covariance and variance under the indicated probability distribution.\\
$F,G,\kappa,P_{ijk}$ & Exact triplet expressions and predicate; $P_{ijk}$
is a logical statement, not a transition matrix.\\
$\tau,u$ & Relaxation time and normalized time $u=t/\tau$ in the physical study;
the letter $u$ has different explicitly defined local uses elsewhere.\\
$\delta,t_x(\delta)$ & A distance threshold and its crossing time.\\
$O(\cdot),o(\cdot)$ & Controlled order of a remainder and a relatively vanishing
remainder, respectively; neither is a blanket finite-error certificate.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\paragraph{The habit to carry forward.}
For every advanced statement, ask what varies and what stays fixed; which
quantity is ordered; whether the comparison is finite-time or asymptotic;
where strictness enters; and how equality or degeneracy is handled.
Those questions are often more useful than memorizing a long formula.
The technical part that follows supplies the proofs, exact data, source
correspondence, implementation details, and explicitly stated open problems.

% Unnumbered teaching material; technical statement numbering is preserved.
\clearpage
\section*{An extended bridge: from random events to a physical certificate}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Extended bridge: from random events to a physical certificate}
The previous lessons supplied the vocabulary. These eight additional lessons
slow down the transition from that vocabulary to the physical computations.
They answer a practical question: how can a finite calculation establish a
statement about an entire interval of times? They then examine what it means
for a mathematical rate to have a physical interpretation.

\section*{Lesson 33. A rate is not a probability: build the clock first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{33. A rate is not a probability}
\paragraph{Begin with units.}
A probability is a number between zero and one. A rate has units of inverse
time. A rate of three events per second is perfectly meaningful; a probability
of three is not. If an event has constant rate $r$, its probability of occurring
in a very short interval of length $h$ is $rh+o(h)$. The notation $o(h)$ means
that the error divided by $h$ tends to zero as $h$ tends to zero. It does not
mean that the approximation is exact for a one-second interval.

\paragraph{Derive the waiting law.}
Let $S(t)$ be the probability that no event has happened by time $t$.
Assume that, conditional on no event so far, the chance in the next short
interval is still $rh+o(h)$. Then
\[
 S(t+h)=S(t)(1-rh+o(h)),\qquad S'(t)=-rS(t),\qquad S(0)=1.
\]
The function with this derivative and initial value is $S(t)=e^{-rt}$.
Thus the probability of at least one event by time $t$ is $1-e^{-rt}$.
For $r=2$ per second and $t=0.1$ seconds, this is $1-e^{-0.2}$,
approximately $0.1813$. The short-time estimate $rt=0.2$ is close but different.
For $t=1$ second the estimate $rt=2$ is not a probability at all.

\paragraph{Why the mean wait is $1/r$.}
The density of the first event time is $re^{-rt}$. Its mean is
\[
 \int_0^\infty t r e^{-rt}\,dt
 =[-te^{-rt}]_0^\infty+\int_0^\infty e^{-rt}\,dt=\frac1r.
\]
The integration by parts has merely moved the factor $t$ onto a derivative.
There is also a geometric interpretation: the mean waiting time is the area
under the survival curve $S(t)$.

\paragraph{Several possible exits.}
From state $j$, let the exit rates to other states be $a_{ij}$.
Independent exponential clocks compete. The probability that none has rung
by $t$ is the product $\prod_{i\ne j}e^{-a_{ij}t}$, hence the total exit rate
is $r_j=\sum_{i\ne j}a_{ij}$. Conditional on an exit, its destination is $i$
with probability $a_{ij}/r_j$. This produces the diagonal entry
$M_{jj}=-r_j$ and explains why a column sums to zero. The negative diagonal
does not describe a negative probability; it describes probability leaving.

\paragraph{Try it.}
Suppose the rates from one state to two others are $2$ and $3$ per second.
The mean residence time is $1/5$ second, and the next destination probabilities
are $2/5$ and $3/5$. Multiplying all rates by ten divides every waiting time
by ten. It does not change these destination probabilities or the equilibrium
distribution. Consequently, a percentage time advantage alone supplies no
absolute number of seconds without a physical rate calibration.

\section*{Lesson 34. Replace unequal clocks by one clock and harmless pauses}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{34. One clock and harmless pauses}
\paragraph{A deliberately simple two-state example.}
Use a column probability vector and let
\[
 M=\begin{pmatrix}-2&1\\2&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \rho=2,\qquad
 P=I+M/\rho=\begin{pmatrix}0&1/2\\1&1/2\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The first state exits at rate $2$; the second exits at rate $1$.
Instead ring a clock of rate $2$ in either state. At each ring, choose the
next state using the current column of $P$. In state two, half the rings
leave the state unchanged. Its actual transition rate is therefore
$2\times(1/2)=1$, exactly as required. The pauses are real elapsed time.

\paragraph{Generalize only after the example.}
Choose $\rho$ at least as large as every exit rate. Then $P=I+M/\rho$
has nonnegative entries and unit column sums. Such a matrix is called
stochastic: it sends probability vectors to probability vectors. The identity
$M=\rho(P-I)$ gives
\[
 e^{Mt}=e^{\rho t(P-I)}
 =e^{-\rho t}\sum_{k=0}^\infty\frac{(\rho t)^k}{k!}P^k.
\]
The factorization is allowed because $P$ commutes with $I$.
It would not be legitimate to split an arbitrary matrix exponential into
two factors without checking commutation.

\paragraph{Read every factor as a probability.}
Write $\mu=\rho t$. A Poisson random variable $N$ has probabilities
$\Pr(N=k)=e^{-\mu}\mu^k/k!$. Here $N$ counts clock rings by time $t$.
Conditional on $N=k$, the distribution is $P^kp(0)$. Averaging these
conditional distributions gives the displayed formula. This procedure is
called uniformization. It is an exact continuous-time construction, not a
small-time-step approximation.

At $\mu=1$, the probabilities of zero, one and two rings are respectively
$e^{-1}$, $e^{-1}$ and $e^{-1}/2$. Their sum is about $0.9197$.
The missing $0.0803$ is the probability of three or more rings; it cannot
simply be discarded when a rigorous error bound is required.

\paragraph{Check the two-state equilibrium.}
Solving $M\pi=0$ and $\pi_1+\pi_2=1$ gives $\pi=(1/3,2/3)^\top$.
Starting from $(1,0)^\top$, the exact first component is
$p_1(t)=1/3+(2/3)e^{-3t}$ and the second is its complement.
Differentiating verifies $p'_1=1-3p_1$, which is also the first row of
$Mp$. This small solvable model is a useful check of the matrix convention,
the clock interpretation and any implementation of uniformization.

\section*{Lesson 35. Bound the part of an infinite sum that you did not compute}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{35. Bound an infinite remainder}
\paragraph{First learn the geometric comparison.}
For $0\leq q<1$, multiplying $1+q+q^2+\cdots$ by $1-q$ cancels every
term except the first, giving $1/(1-q)$. If a nonnegative series starts with
$a$ and every later term is at most $q$ times its predecessor, its whole sum
is at most $a/(1-q)$. Equality is unnecessary; an upper bound suffices.

For Poisson weights $w_k=e^{-\mu}\mu^k/k!$,
\[
 \frac{w_{k+1}}{w_k}=\frac{\mu}{k+1}.
\]
After keeping terms $0$ through $K$, the first omitted weight is $w_{K+1}$.
Every subsequent ratio is at most $q=\mu/(K+2)$. Provided $q<1$,
\[
 R_K=\sum_{k=K+1}^\infty w_k
 \leq\frac{w_{K+1}}{1-\mu/(K+2)}.
\]
The $K+2$ in the denominator is intentional: it bounds the ratio from the
first omitted term to the next omitted term. Confusing it with the first
omitted term's index is an easy off-by-one mistake.

\paragraph{Work a numerical example.}
Take $\mu=1$ and keep $K=2$. Then $w_3=e^{-1}/6$ and $q=1/4$, so
$R_2\leq 2e^{-1}/9$, approximately $0.08175$. The actual missing mass
is approximately $0.08030$, below the bound. The bound is a little wider
than necessary, but its validity is transparent.

\paragraph{Turn a scalar tail into a vector guarantee.}
Every component of $P^kp(0)$ lies in $[0,1]$. If $s_i$ is the computed
partial sum for component $i$, then $p_i(t)\in[s_i,s_i+R_K]$.
These independent intervals need not sum to exactly one; they are an
enclosure of a probability vector, not themselves a new probability vector.
Renormalizing their endpoints without a proof could invalidate the enclosure.

\paragraph{Include arithmetic error as well as truncation error.}
An exact tail formula does not excuse rounding errors in the partial sum.
The nominal checker computes interval bounds for inputs, exponentials,
matrix products and logarithms. If the entire probability interval is
positive, it can enclose each term $p_i\log(p_i/\pi_i)$ and hence KL.
An interval crossing zero requires a valid alternative treatment or a
failure to certify; a logarithm of an invalid endpoint is not evidence.

In the replayed nominal calculation, at most 281 terms were needed and the
largest reported tail upper bound was below $10^{-45}$. The small tail is
one part of the certificate. The validity of $P$, the input correspondence,
the outward arithmetic and the later comparison inequalities remain
separate necessary parts. More printed digits alone establish none of them.

\section*{Lesson 36. Prove a whole time interval using its endpoints}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{36. From endpoints to continuous time}
\paragraph{A descending staircase picture.}
Suppose two distances $D_c(t)$ and $D_h(t)$ are nonincreasing in time.
On a cell $[a,b]$, the cold distance is at least its value at the right
endpoint, while the hot distance is at most its value at the left endpoint:
\[
 D_c(t)\geq D_c(b),\qquad D_h(t)\leq D_h(a).
\]
Therefore a single strict inequality
\[
 D_h(a)<0.9D_c(b)
\]
proves $D_h(t)<0.9D_c(t)$ for every time in that cell.
The endpoints are deliberately mismatched to use the worst case for each
curve. Merely checking $D_h(a)<0.9D_c(a)$ and the analogous inequality at
$b$ would not control a crossing between them.

\paragraph{Why KL decreases here.}
For a stochastic transition matrix $K$ and positive equilibrium $\pi$,
let $r_i=p_i/\pi_i$. When $K\pi=\pi$, the output likelihood ratio is
\[
 \frac{(Kp)_j}{\pi_j}=\sum_i\frac{K_{ji}\pi_i}{\pi_j}r_i.
\]
The coefficients in this sum are nonnegative and sum to one. Convexity of
$f(r)=r\log r$ says that $f$ of this weighted average is no greater than
the weighted average of $f$. Multiply by $\pi_j$, sum over $j$, and use
$\sum_jK_{ji}=1$ to obtain
\[
 D(Kp\Vert\pi)\leq D(p\Vert\pi).
\]
Apply this to $K=e^{M(b-a)}$. This argument proves the monotonicity used
above. It does not say that $D_h(t)/D_c(t)$ is monotone.

\paragraph{A hand-checkable cell.}
Suppose outward bounds give $D_h(a)\leq0.0080$ and
$D_c(b)\geq0.0090$. Then $0.9D_c(b)-D_h(a)\geq0.0001>0$.
Every interior time passes the ten-percent comparison. If the computed
margin interval were $[-0.0001,0.0002]$, no conclusion would follow;
a narrower cell might resolve it.

\paragraph{Join the cells and check the floor.}
A finite list $t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_N$ covers the entire closed interval
$[t_0,t_N]$ without gaps. Proving the endpoint inequality on every cell
proves the full window. Since cold KL decreases, a lower bound
$D_c(t_N)\geq10^{-3}$ also proves that floor throughout the window.
The nominal replay used 80 cells and a minimum certified comparison margin
greater than $0.0004408$. Its fixed endpoints were approximately
$58.89269$ and $117.78539$ in attempt-time units. The script separately
encloses the slow time to justify the window-length requirement; a decimal
time chosen from a numerical eigenvalue does not become exact merely by
being described as a multiple of that numerical estimate.

\section*{Lesson 37. Prove a whole band of target distances}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{37. A whole band of target distances}
\paragraph{Reverse the graph in your mind.}
For a positive target $\delta$ below the initial distance, define
$t_x(\delta)$ as the first time preparation $x$ reaches that target.
A smaller target means getting closer to equilibrium, so it takes longer.
Thus if $\delta_a<\delta_b$, then $t_x(\delta_a)\geq t_x(\delta_b)$.
For the irreducible reversible model and a nonequilibrium preparation,
KL strictly decreases until its limiting equilibrium value; positive targets
below the initial value have unique crossings.

\paragraph{A root estimate proposes; a sign bracket verifies.}
A numerical solver might propose $t=10.1234$. To verify a bracket $[L,U]$,
enclose $D(L)-\delta$ strictly above zero and $D(U)-\delta$ strictly below
zero. Continuity gives a crossing between them; strict decrease gives
uniqueness. An approximate residual close to zero is weaker than these
two certified signs. We also need $0<L<U$ for the ratio bound below.

\paragraph{Choose the unfavorable corners.}
For every $\delta\in[\delta_a,\delta_b]$,
\[
 t_h(\delta)\leq t_h(\delta_a)\leq U_h(\delta_a),\qquad
 t_c(\delta)\geq t_c(\delta_b)\geq L_c(\delta_b)>0.
\]
Division by a positive denominator preserves the intended order, giving
\[
 1-\frac{t_h(\delta)}{t_c(\delta)}
 \geq 1-\frac{U_h(\delta_a)}{L_c(\delta_b)}.
\]
If the right side exceeds $0.05$, every target in that cell has at least
five-percent time saving. Notice that the numerator and denominator use
opposite ends of the target cell. This conservative mismatch is exactly
what covers its interior.

\paragraph{Try the ratio with ordinary numbers.}
Suppose the hottest trajectory's slowest permitted arrival is at most
$90$ seconds and the cold trajectory's fastest permitted arrival is at
least $100$ seconds. The saving is at least $1-90/100=10\%$.
If the denominator were instead only known to exceed $92$, the bound would
fall to about $2.17\%$, even if all sampled same-target comparisons looked
better. The proof must use the bounds it has actually established.

The nominal certificate covers $[10^{-3},10^{-2}]$ with 60 target cells.
Its conservative lower bound on time saving is approximately $8.12245\%$.
This is a guaranteed lower bound for that nominal model, not the exact
minimum. The later eight-obligation certificate handles the full uncertain
four-state parameter box. It is a distinct proof, not a conclusion obtained
by silently extrapolating the nominal calculation.

\section*{Lesson 38. Derive a transition rate from an energy exchange}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{38. From energy exchange to transition rate}
\paragraph{Begin with one uphill move.}
Let two system energies be $E_j=1$ and $E_i=3$, with barrier energy $B=5$.
Draw a nonnegative reservoir energy $Y$ with density $\beta e^{-\beta Y}$.
The move from $j$ is allowed when $1+Y\geq5$, namely when $Y\geq4$.
At acceptance, set $Y'=Y+1-3=Y-2$. It is nonnegative, and
$E_i+Y'=3+Y-2=1+Y=E_j+Y$: combined energy is conserved.
The reverse contact sees the same total energy and can undo the move.

\paragraph{Integrate the acceptance probability.}
For any $c\geq0$,
\[
 \Pr(Y\geq c)=\int_c^\infty\beta e^{-\beta y}\,dy=e^{-\beta c}.
\]
If contacts for this pair arrive at rate $\nu_0$, the accepted rate from
$j$ to $i$ is therefore
\[
 a_{ij}=\nu_0e^{-\beta(B-E_j)}.
\]
The reverse rate is $a_{ji}=\nu_0e^{-\beta(B-E_i)}$.
Their ratio is $e^{-\beta(E_i-E_j)}$. Multiplying by Gibbs weights
gives the stronger flux identity
\[
 a_{ij}e^{-\beta E_j}=a_{ji}e^{-\beta E_i}
 =\nu_0e^{-\beta B},
\]
which is detailed balance before the common normalization is inserted.

\paragraph{Separate an always-required gate from the uphill cost.}
Write $h=B-\max(E_i,E_j)$. Since
$B-E_j=h+\max(E_i-E_j,0)$, the rate becomes
\[
 a_{ij}=\nu_0e^{-\beta h}\min\{1,e^{-\beta(E_i-E_j)}\}.
\]
In the example at $\beta=1$, the common gate contributes $e^{-2}$,
the uphill acceptance contributes another $e^{-2}$, and their product is
$e^{-4}$. The reverse move has only the common $e^{-2}$ gate.

\paragraph{Why simulation may omit attempts but not their time.}
One can retain only contacts with $Y\geq h$. Their clock has rate
$\nu_0e^{-\beta h}$. Conditional on passing the gate,
\[
 \Pr(Y-h\geq z\mid Y\geq h)
 =\frac{e^{-\beta(h+z)}}{e^{-\beta h}}=e^{-\beta z}.
\]
Thus a retained mark is $h$ plus a fresh exponential energy. This proves
the thinning rule used in the event simulation. Counting every attempted
contact as an accepted transition would alter the process; ignoring
rejected contacts without adjusting the clock would alter physical time.

\paragraph{What this construction establishes.}
Under independent fresh thermal marks, independent Poisson pair clocks,
constant reservoir density of energy states, and instantaneous updates,
the rate identity holds exactly for every allowed barrier and energy.
It supplies a stochastic bath interpretation of the same four-state model.
It does not identify a material or derive an autonomous microscopic bath.
The replayed simulation of 500,000 trajectories per preparation agrees
with direct evolution at five times to within $0.00118$ per component.
That is a diagnostic check of the simulation; the rate identity above is
the reason the mathematical generators coincide.

\section*{Lesson 39. An intermediate state introduces a second clock}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{39. Intermediate states and two clocks}
\paragraph{Insert a waiting room between two wells.}
Suppose the desired well-to-well rates are $u$ from $j$ to $i$ and $v$
from $i$ to $j$. Insert state $s$. Use entrance rates $2u$ from $j$
and $2v$ from $i$, and exit rate $b$ from $s$ to each well. On entering
$s$, the next well is selected with probabilities $1/2$ and $1/2$.
Half the visits return to the well just left.

If the clock is stopped during every visit to $s$, the successful transition
rate from $j$ to $i$ is $2u\times(1/2)=u$. This is the trace clock:
only time actually spent in the wells is retained. With ordinary physical
time, each visit adds a mean wait $1/(2b)$. Those waits cannot be removed
when claiming a physical completion time.

\paragraph{Derive the block identity one entry at a time.}
Order the wells as $(j,i)$. Their direct block, the entrance block, the
exit block and the intermediate block are
\[
 Q_{ww}=\begin{pmatrix}-2u&0\\0&-2v\end{pmatrix},\quad
 Q_{sw}=\begin{pmatrix}2u&2v\end{pmatrix},\quad
 Q_{ws}=\begin{pmatrix}b\\b\end{pmatrix},\quad Q_{ss}=-2b.
\]
The subscripts give destination first, as everywhere else. Multiplication
now gives
\[
 Q_{ww}-Q_{ws}Q_{ss}^{-1}Q_{sw}
 =\begin{pmatrix}-2u&0\\0&-2v\end{pmatrix}
  +\begin{pmatrix}u&v\\u&v\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}-u&v\\u&-v\end{pmatrix}.
\]
This is exactly the intended two-well generator. Adding the six pair
contributions gives the four-well identity in the technical report.
This exact algebra identifies the trace generator; it does not assert that
the physical-time well probabilities solve that same four-state equation.

\paragraph{Equilibrium and preparation also change.}
In the expanded model, each intermediate has energy $B$ and phase-space
weight $\eta>0$. Its equilibrium weight is $\eta e^{-B}$, compared
with $e^{-E_j}$ for a well. Taking $b=2/\eta$ in attempt-time units
gives mean intermediate residence time $\eta/4$ and matched equilibrium
fluxes. At preparation temperature $T$, all ten states must be prepared
with weights $g_a e^{-E_a/T}$. Preparing only the wells would be a different
experiment. The phase-space weight $\eta$ need not be an integer count.

At $\eta=0.05$, the nominal cold and hot preparations place about $1.90\%$
and $4.07\%$ of their mass in intermediates. Full ten-state calculations
retain those populations and every residence time. Their finite-time
advantage persists at the tested points. The expanded system also has
higher energies and faster rates outside the original all-state caps.
It is a separate test of a modeling assumption, not a new certificate
inside the original constrained four-state class.

\section*{Lesson 40. Read a computational result at the right strength}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{40. Read the evidence at the right strength}
\paragraph{Four questions before accepting a result.}
What object was calculated? What inputs were used? Which errors were bounded?
Which variables were covered continuously? An answer such as ``70 digits''
only addresses numerical precision, and even then does not itself establish
a bound on every implementation or modeling error.

The independent ten-state calculation directly constructs and exponentiates
the full generator at 70-digit working precision. It agrees with the fast
spectral implementation within $6.55\times10^{-13}$ in KL at five tested
times. Different formulas agreeing is useful evidence against a numerical
implementation mistake. Neither implementation uses outward error bounds
for that comparison, so it is a high-precision diagnostic, not an interval
certificate.

\paragraph{Interpret a large sample precisely.}
For each of three fixed $\eta$ values, the study tests 256 corners and 500
seeded interior parameter points. The total is $3(256+500)=2268$.
Each point is further tested on finite target and time grids. No sampled
case fails the declared benchmarks. This says exactly what happened on
those grids. It does not exclude a narrow failure between points, between
time samples, between targets, or at an untested value of $\eta$.
The continuous four-state certificate cannot be transferred to this
ten-state extension merely because their trace generators agree.

\paragraph{A percentage needs a denominator and a clock.}
At $\eta=0.05$ and target $10^{-3}$, the independently computed arrival
times are approximately $210.67249$ for cold and $190.05131$ for hot.
Using the cold time as denominator gives
\[
 \frac{210.67249-190.05131}{210.67249}\approx0.0978826,
\]
or about $9.78826\%$. Dividing by the hot time would answer a different
percentage question. These are attempt-time units, not calibrated seconds.
The calculation includes post-quench intermediate residence times but does
not include the cost or duration of preparing the hotter initial state.

\paragraph{Know what a program's success message establishes.}
A zero exit code means that the process completed without a reported
execution failure. Some exploratory programs record failures in JSON
without returning a nonzero code. Read the recorded failure counts, input
hashes, unresolved cells, parameter scope and numerical residuals. A
certificate runner must also reject incomplete coverage. A numerical
search that found nothing has a different meaning from a complete proof
that nothing exists.

\paragraph{The final handoff to advanced mathematics.}
You can now read the physical section by following a chain of explicit
claims: construct valid rates; establish equilibrium; evolve preparations;
bound numerical error; prove continuous comparisons; and interpret the
clock and physical assumptions. The advanced spectral and interval
methods make that chain efficient over an uncertain parameter box.
They do not replace any link. When a later formula looks forbidding,
identify which link it serves before trying to manipulate its symbols.


\clearpage
\part*{Part II. Technical research report and exact certificates}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part II. Technical research report and exact certificates}
\noindent The abstract below summarizes the research results in their technical
language. All original numbered sections, theorems, equations, and proof text
are retained after this teaching part; the foundational examples above use
unnumbered displays so that those references keep their original numbering.
\par\bigskip

\begin{abstract}
We disprove the four-state finite-temperature triplet conjecture of Avitan, Factor and Gelbwaser-Klimovsky with a strictly positive reversible generator and an exact certificate. Under the conjecture's original triplet premise, the ordinary cooling amplitude advantage has sharp supremum $25\%$. A uniformly gapped family approaches this limit with all triplet inequalities strict. Cooling occurs exactly when an endpoint covariance is positive; an equivalent rate test uses one cubic root. Every repeated-slow four-state generator under the premise is a reset plus mixing on one adjacent pair and has normal convex-divergence ordering at every time. In arbitrary dimension, the triplet premise excludes strong effects and asymptotic heating, yet permits ordinary cooling advantages approaching $1-3/n$. Thus no dimension-independent amplitude bound below one survives. A weighted prefix-contrast representation also yields finite-time heating exclusion in weighted Euclidean distance, a uniform finite-time relative-entropy ordering window on both bath sides, and all-temperature heating order for every convex divergence in a structured generator family. For arbitrary simple four-state slow modes, an explicit Gibbs Wronskian identity gives a finite asymptotic classification; degenerate modes have a separate distance-specific test. Exact executable classifications cover commensurate rational systems and, under the triplet premise, rational rates without commensurate energies. We distinguish these proved results from unresolved general finite-time questions and the higher-dimensional sharp bound. Lean verifies a rational-mode counterexample, its eventual Pearson chi-square relaxation reversal, and the sharp four-state amplitude supremum; the formalization has explicitly delimited coverage and reproducible build instructions. Classical order, spectral, total-positivity, and transcendence tools are explicitly attributed.
\end{abstract}

\section{Outcome}
The proposed triplet test can miss a genuine four-state relaxation reversal. In the example, the hotter preparation begins farther from equilibrium, yet later becomes slightly closer. The reversal is mathematically rigorous and physically small. Every input rate and bath weight is rational. The energies are logarithms of those weights. A cubic with integer coefficients specifies the slow eigenvalue, and cofactor polynomials specify its left eigenvector exactly. Thermal probabilities at the two selected temperatures are algebraic numbers bounded by rational numbers whose correctness is checked by integer powers.

A companion example has an exactly rational slow eigenvalue and left eigenvector. It gives a second, simpler spectral certificate, at the expense of two less compact rates. Both examples are strictly inside the required positive-rate and simple-slow-mode domain. Section~\ref{sec:mechanism} extends the companion into a parameter family that crosses a proved cooling threshold while all triplet tests continue to fail. No assertion about a strong Mpemba effect is needed: the two displayed slow-mode overlaps are nonzero.

Sections~\ref{sec:fulltripletbound} and~\ref{sec:tripletendpoint} establish the sharp $25\%$ amplitude supremum and a single-endpoint cooling criterion under the original triplet premise. The new exclusion theorem in Section~\ref{sec:orderedconductances} shows that the counterexample has a restricted mechanism: it reverses ordinary cooling while retaining a nonzero slow overlap. The same triplet premise rules out strong effects in every dimension and rules out asymptotic heating. Section~\ref{sec:universal} supplies a full finite asymptotic classification for arbitrary simple four-state slow modes; Section~\ref{sec:degenerateextension} states the distance-specific extension to other slow multiplicities. Theorem~\ref{thm:tripletdegenerate} completes the full-premise degenerate boundary: reset plus one adjacent pair is necessary, and excludes all-time convex-divergence reversals. The same classification holds in every dimension $n\geq4$ when there is just one faster mode. Corollary~\ref{cor:rateendpoint} implements the simple-mode cooling decision without an eigenvector. These conclusions have mathematical proofs rather than being inferred from the discovery searches.

The Lean companion verifies the rational-mode example in \hyperref[app:rational]{Appendix B}, an initial-to-eventual reversal in Pearson chi-square distance under its master equation, and the sharp four-state amplitude supremum. Section~\ref{sec:lean} maps these claims to named theorems and gives reproduction commands. Its sharpness proof uses a simpler family with two triplet equalities; the report's stronger uniform-gap and all-strict perturbation claims are outside that formal coverage.

\section{Problem and literature status}
The target is the inference that failure of Eq.~(3) on every energy-ordered triplet of a four-state reversible thermalizing generator precludes the Mpemba effect. The triplets retain the original off-diagonal rates. This is the conjecture in \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04567v1}{Avitan, Factor and Gelbwaser-Klimovsky, arXiv:2603.04567v1}, submitted 4 March 2026. Its main text and supporting information, particularly S2--S14, were checked on 11 October 2026. The version history listed only v1. Its finite-temperature claim is numerical; the analytic theorem assumes zero bath temperature and stronger triplet inequalities.

The later \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2609.09988v1}{Gelbwaser-Klimovsky, arXiv:2609.09988v1}, submitted 9 September 2026, repeats the inference without providing a general proof. Its examples without an exact three-state mechanism concern a different, stronger predicate. Searches by title, identifier, conjecture, correction and counterexample located no other primary-source resolution. This establishes the scope of the literature check; it does not exclude unindexed or unpublished work. The source-to-counterexample correspondence is made explicit in Section~\ref{sec:claimscope}. Publication priority or exhaustive novelty is not claimed.

For supporting no-Mpemba results we additionally checked \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2410.06623v2}{Ohga, Hayakawa and Ito, arXiv:2410.06623v2}, version-history date 19 October 2024, including Result 2 and SI B--D, on 11 October 2026. Their theorem is a prior application of monotone coupling to absence of mean-energy crossings. Section~\ref{sec:priororder} compares the assumptions and observables precisely. The broader assessment in Section~\ref{sec:novelty} also checks Fill--Kahn (v3, 6 September 2013), Baake--Sumner (v3, 25 September 2022), and Vu--Hayakawa (v2, 6 January 2025), including their relevant full-text arguments. Supporting applications carry no independent novelty claim.

For definitions we use \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5441807/}{Lu and Raz, PNAS 114, 5083--5088 (2017)}, and distinguish the strong effect of \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.05829}{Klich et al., Phys. Rev. X 9, 021060 (2019)}. The asymptotic conclusion is supplemented below by certified finite-time crossing brackets.

\subsection{Model and reductions}
With column probabilities, $\dot p=Mp$, $M_{ij}=a_{ij}$ for $i\ne j$, and $M_{jj}=-\sum_{i\ne j}a_{ij}$. The rate $a_{ij}$ goes from $j$ to $i$. Write
\[
q_i=e^{-\beta_b E_i},\qquad Q=\sum_iq_i,\qquad \pi_b=q/Q.
\]
Detailed balance is $M_{ij}q_j=M_{ji}q_i$. An energy shift sets $E_1=0$; measuring energies in bath units sets $\beta_b=1$. A common positive multiplier of $M$ changes only the time unit. We use $a_{12}=1$. We neither assume equally spaced energies nor normalize the total energy span. These would impose additional restrictions.

If $D=\diag(\pi_b)$, detailed balance makes $S=D^{-1/2}MD^{1/2}$ symmetric: $MD=DM^\top$. Hence the spectrum is real and the generator is diagonalizable. Its Dirichlet form is
\[
-f^\top D M^\top f=\sum_{i<j}c_{ij}(f_i-f_j)^2,\qquad c_{ij}=M_{ij}\pi_{bj}=c_{ji}.
\]
In the positive-rate domain the stationary eigenvalue is simple. The counterexample and scalar-overlap classification additionally assume a simple slowest nonzero eigenvalue. Section~\ref{sec:degenerateextension} treats the other multiplicities for specified asymptotic distances, while Theorem~\ref{thm:orderedk} does not require simplicity.

\subsection{Exact predicate and its negation}
For $i<j<k$, put $A=a_{ik}$, $B=a_{jk}$, $C=a_{ij}$, $t=q_j/q_i$, $u=q_k/q_j$. The source's $(-,0,+)$ correspond to $(i,j,k)$. Its exact test is
\begin{align*}
\kappa&=\frac{A+B}{C(1+t)},&G&=At-B,\\
F&=A(1+tu/2)-B(1+u/2)-C(1-t),\\
P_{ijk}&=(\kappa\geq1\ \land\ F>0)\ \lor\ (\kappa\leq1\ \land\ G>0).
\end{align*}
Both strict comparisons and both inclusive branch comparisons match Eq.~(3) and S9. This is a direct transcription of the published test, using destination-first rate indices.

\begin{lemma}[The branch logic reduces to a convex cone]
For positive $C$ and $0<t<1$, $\neg P_{ijk}$ is equivalent to $F\leq0$ and $G\leq0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $H=A+B-C(1+t)$, $d=(1-t)/(1+t)>0$, and $h=2/(1+t)+u/2>0$. Direct expansion gives $F=hG+dH$. If $H>0$, failure is $F\leq0$, which forces $G<0$. If $H<0$, failure is $G\leq0$, which forces $F<0$. At $H=0$ both branches apply, but $F=hG$, so their strict tests agree. These cases prove both directions.
\end{proof}
For fixed energies the eight linear inequalities define one closed polyhedral cone. The admissible rates are its intersection with the strictly positive orthant. This conclusion does not discard the original branch logic; it proves an equivalent formulation including $\kappa=1$.

The supporting-information fast-vector formulas divide by $-uG$. We do not use that division. When $G=0$, direct triplet diagonalization gives nonzero eigenvalues $-A(1+t+tu)$ and $-[C(1+t)+Atu]$. If also $H=0$, they coincide. The predicate remains well-defined whenever $C>0$. Isolated triplet generators require their diagonals to be recomputed, not inherited as principal submatrices.

\subsection{Precise published claim and the contradiction}\label{sec:claimscope}
The target is the finite-temperature conjecture in Avitan, Factor and Gelbwaser-Klimovsky, rather than an extrapolation supplied by this report. Their \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04567v1#Sx3}{N-level protocol} states: ``if all triplets violate the conditions, the $N$-level system will not exhibit the Mpemba effect.'' The \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04567v1#Sx3.SSx2}{non-zero-temperature subsection} explicitly identifies this as a conjecture. The protocol and SI Eqs.~(S10)--(S14) specify the original rates.

Let $\mathsf{ME}_{\rm cool}(M,E,\beta_b)$ mean that there exist finite $0<\beta_h<\beta_c<\beta_b$ with $|b_2(\beta_h)|<|b_2(\beta_c)|$, where the bath and generator are fixed and the slow nonzero eigenvalue is simple. Section~\ref{sec:cooling} shows that this implies an initial-to-eventual KL-distance reversal. A necessary consequence of the published conjecture, restricted to four states and this regular domain, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:claimC4}
\boxed{\quad
\bigwedge_{i<j<k}\neg P_{ijk}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\neg\mathsf{ME}_{\rm cool}(M,E,\beta_b).
\quad}\tag{$\mathcal C_4$}
\end{equation}
This is a universally quantified implication over distinct ordered energies, positive reversible rates and $0<\beta_b<\infty$. Requiring positive rates and a simple slow mode restricts the domain; it does not enlarge the authors' claim. Restricting the conclusion to cooling makes it weaker. A counterexample to this restricted consequence therefore suffices to refute the unrestricted finite-temperature conjecture, independently of any heating or spectral-boundary convention.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{p{.42\textwidth}p{.49\textwidth}}
\toprule Requirement in $(\mathcal C_4)$&Witness in Theorem~\ref{thm:counterexample}\\\midrule
Four ordered distinct energies&$E=(0,\log(200/199),\log(5/2),\log400)$.\\
Finite positive bath&$\beta_b=1$.\\
Conservative reversible thermal dynamics&All twelve off-diagonal rates are positive; $\mathbf1^\top M=0$ and $M\diag(q)$ is symmetric.\\
Simple slow nonzero eigenvalue&One root in each of $(-20,-19)$, $(-60,-59)$ and $(-337,-336)$.\\
Original-rate triplet failures&Exact evaluations below give $F<0$ and $G<0$ on all four triplets, with both $\kappa$ branches represented.\\
Conclusion contradicted&$\beta_h=1/1000<\beta_c=1/100<1$ and $|b_2(\beta_c)|-|b_2(\beta_h)|>0.0001034076$.\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The table records the proof obligations, which the exact construction below discharges. No effective rate, principal-submatrix diagonal, infinite initial temperature or equality case is used. Shifting all energies by the same positive constant also makes $E_1>0$, if desired, without changing any rate test or Gibbs distribution.

\paragraph{Three distinct statements.}
The March paper's zero-temperature theorem assumes $\beta_b\to\infty$, a nondegenerate spectrum and $C>A+B$ for every triplet (SI Eq.~S20). Our bath is finite and the latter inequality fails already for $123$: $1<28+1799/8$. Thus the counterexample does not challenge that theorem or the three-state necessity of Eq.~(3).

The September paper, \href{https://arxiv.org/html/2609.09988v1#S2}{Gelbwaser-Klimovsky, Section II}, distinguishes Eq.~(3) from its exact three-state mechanisms. Write $\mathcal E_{ijk}$ for satisfaction of one of those mechanisms. In their nondegenerate three-state setting, necessity gives $\mathcal E_{ijk}\Rightarrow P_{ijk}$, but not conversely. Consequently
\[
\bigwedge\neg P_{ijk}\ \Longrightarrow\ \bigwedge\neg\mathcal E_{ijk}.
\]
Necessity supplies no converse to this implication. Its reported four-state effects with no triplet mechanism therefore do not, by that fact alone, establish failure of $(\mathcal C_4)$. Section II separately reiterates the original-rate Eq.~(3) absence inference. Our witness verifies the stronger premise $\bigwedge\neg P_{ijk}$ explicitly.

\paragraph{General conjecture versus sampled parameter ranges.}
The March Fig.~2 uses rates from $0.05$ to $1$ and $\beta_bE_i$ from $0$ to $1$; the September Fig.~5 uses rates from $0.001$ to $1$ and $\beta_bE_i$ from $0$ to $5$. These are sampling ranges, not hypotheses attached to the general inference. Our invariant energy span is $\beta_b(E_4-E_1)=\log400>5$, outside both boxes. Even the downward-rate ratio $a_{12}/a_{23}=8/1799<0.05$ prevents a common time rescaling from placing all downward rates in the March box. We therefore refute the unrestricted conjecture; we neither reproduce nor contradict the finite sample counts, and make no claim of a counterexample within either sampled box.

\subsection{What the cooling witness proves}\label{sec:cooling}
Choose $Mv_2=\lambda_2v_2$, $w_2^\top M=\lambda_2w_2^\top$ and $w_2^\top v_2=1$. Since $\lambda_2\ne0$, $w_2^\top\pi_b=0$. Thus
\[
b_2(\beta)=w_2^\top[\pi(\beta)-\pi_b]=w_2^\top\pi(\beta).
\]
All initial temperatures here are finite and positive. Cooling has $0<\beta_h<\beta_c<\beta_b$, with anomalous ordering $|b_2(\beta_h)|<|b_2(\beta_c)|$. Heating instead has $\beta_b<\beta_h<\beta_c$ and requires $|b_2(\beta_c)|<|b_2(\beta_h)|$.

With a simple slow mode,
\[
p(t;\beta)-\pi_b=e^{\lambda_2t}b_2(\beta)v_2+O(e^{\lambda_3t}).
\]
Consequently a strict overlap comparison gives the same eventual comparison in every fixed norm. For relative entropy,
\[
D_{\rm KL}(p(t;\beta)\Vert\pi_b)=\tfrac12e^{2\lambda_2t}b_2(\beta)^2
\sum_i v_{2i}^2/\pi_{bi}+o(e^{2\lambda_2t}).
\]
The hotter initial Gibbs state is initially farther in this distance because
\[
\frac{d}{d\beta}D_{\rm KL}(\pi(\beta)\Vert\pi_b)
=(\beta-\beta_b)\Var_{\pi(\beta)}(E).
\]
Thus the example gives a genuine initial-to-eventual distance reversal. It does not claim reversal at every time or for arbitrary distance functionals without appropriate hypotheses. A strong effect would require a nonbath preparation with zero slow overlap; that is not the witness used here.

Holding the bath, generator and eigenvectors fixed gives
\[
b_2'(\beta)=-\Cov_{\pi(\beta)}(w_2,E)
=-\sum_{i<j}\pi_i(\beta)\pi_j(\beta)(w_{2j}-w_{2i})(E_j-E_i).
\]
A stationary point alone does not prove a maximum. We instead certify two strict overlap values on the same bath side. Since $b_2(1)=0$, their increasing absolute ordering also forces an interior maximum somewhere between $\beta_h$ and the bath.

\section{Main result and exact counterexample}
\begin{theorem}[Counterexample to the published finite-temperature inference]\label{thm:counterexample}
There exist four distinct ordered energies, a finite positive bath temperature and a strictly positive reversible generator with a simple slowest nonzero eigenvalue such that all four original-rate predicates $P_{ijk}$ fail, yet two finite cooling preparations obey $|b_2(\beta_h)|<|b_2(\beta_c)|$. Thus the antecedent of $(\mathcal C_4)$ holds and its conclusion is false.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Set
\[
\beta_b=1,\quad q=(1,199/200,2/5,1/400),\quad
E=(0,\log(200/199),\log(5/2),\log400).
\]
These energies are distinct and strictly ordered. The downward rates in the order $(12,13,14,23,24,34)$ are
\[
(1,28,249/10,1799/8,4983/200,251/25).
\]
Set upward rates by $a_{ji}=a_{ij}q_j/q_i$ for $i<j$. The full generator is
\[
M=\begin{pmatrix}
-49029/4000&1&28&249/10\\
199/200&-7280583/79600&1799/8&4983/200\\
56/5&17990/199&-1011751/4000&251/25\\
249/4000&4983/79600&251/4000&-11971/200
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Every off-diagonal entry is positive. Its columns sum to zero and $M\diag(q)$ is symmetric. Since $Q=959/400$, the equilibrium is $\pi_b=(400,398,160,1)/959$.

Exact original-rate triplet evaluations are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule Triplet&$\kappa$&$F$&$G$\\\midrule
123&$7225/57$&$-1176493/4975$&$-39403/200$\\
124&$3321/133$&$-32119/1592000$&$-279/2000$\\
134&$1747/1960$&$-7761/4000$&$-2/25$\\
234&$1391209/12548025$&$-190400119/1592000$&$-119/4975$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All four predicates fail strictly, with two upper branches and two lower branches. No boundary convention is needed.

The characteristic polynomial is $xC(x)/1273600000000$, where
\begin{align*}
C(x)={}&1273600000000x^3+530473008000000x^2\\
&+35732953114072400x+510539126345027563.
\end{align*}
Exact opposite endpoint signs show one root in each of $(-337,-336)$, $(-60,-59)$ and $(-20,-19)$. These three disjoint sign-changing intervals exhaust a cubic's roots; all are simple. The largest, denoted $r$, is $\lambda_2$. Approximately,
\[
(\lambda_2,\lambda_3,\lambda_4)=(-19.86836952145,-59.91747997894,-336.72876105237).
\]
In particular the slow-mode spectral gap exceeds 39 in these time units.

Let $P_i(x)$ be the explicit cofactor polynomials listed in \hyperref[app:cofactors]{Appendix A}. Their common factor corresponds to the first column of $\operatorname{adj}(M^\top-xI)$. Put $w_i=P_i(r)/P_1(r)$. The nonvanishing denominator is certified by the rational isolating interval below. The adjugate identity proves $M^\top w=rw$. It also follows that $q^\top w=0$. To impose the required biorthogonal normalization set
\[
N=\sum_iq_iw_i^2>0,\qquad v_i=q_iw_i/N.
\]
Detailed balance gives $Mv=rv$ and $w^\top v=1$. Thus $w=w_2$, $v=v_2$ is a valid consistently normalized pair. Approximately,
\[
w=(1,-0.75893729814493,-0.61211930644096,-0.0038663077658171).
\]
The relevant overlap is exactly
\[
b_2(\beta)=\frac{\sum_iw_iq_i^\beta}{\sum_iq_i^\beta}.
\]
For $\beta_h=1/1000$ and $\beta_c=1/100$, rational interval certification gives the following deliberately widened enclosures:
\begin{align*}
-0.0937454280&<b_2(\beta_h)<-0.0937454279,\\
-0.0938488357&<b_2(\beta_c)<-0.0938488356.
\end{align*}
Every displayed terminating decimal denotes an exact rational number. In particular
\[
|b_2(\beta_c)|-|b_2(\beta_h)|>0.0001034076>0.
\]
Both preparations have finite temperatures, $T_h=1000$ and $T_c=100$ in units with $k_B=1$, versus $T_b=1$. The strict overlap comparison and spectral expansion establish the ordinary cooling Mpemba effect. The verification construction below proves the enclosures without relying on floating-point acceptance tests. This completes the counterexample.
\end{proof}

\section{Mathematical explanation and supporting applications}
\subsection{Why the triplet inference fails}
The highest-energy state's slow-mode value is almost zero, while the intermediate states have strongly negative values. At very high initial temperature, occupation of state 4 therefore dilutes the negative slow overlap. The slightly cooler preparation depopulates state 4 enough to increase the magnitude of that overlap. At still lower temperatures, transfer toward the positive ground-state component returns the overlap to zero at the bath. This explanation concerns projection onto a relaxation mode, not merely mean energy.

An isolated triplet has a different eigenvector and a different equilibrium normalization from the full four-state system. Pairwise detailed balance and the inequalities $F,G<0$ do not control the orientation of the full slow eigenvector relative to the entire Gibbs curve. In particular, nonmonotone slow eigenvectors are permitted. This explains the missing implication in a triplet-only argument.

All rates, $F$ and $G$ are strict; the slow eigenvalue is separated; the overlap gap is strict. Continuous dependence of a simple eigenprojection, Gibbs probabilities and the predicates on rates and energies therefore yields an open neighborhood of counterexamples, including varying energies. This is not an equality-surface artifact. No numerical radius for that neighborhood is claimed.

There is also an explicit one-parameter family. Let $R_0=\pi_b\mathbf1^\top-I$ and $M_\rho=M+\rho R_0$ for $\rho\geq0$. Its off-diagonal reset rates are $\rho\pi_{bi}$, for which $F=G=0$ on every triplet. Thus $F,G$ remain unchanged. On the zero-sum subspace $R_0=-I$, so every nonzero eigenvalue shifts by $-\rho$ and every mode and overlap is preserved. Positivity and the counterexample persist for all $\rho\geq0$. A subsequent common positive time rescaling also preserves the result. More explicitly, for every $\alpha>24$, the same $q$ and the downward rates
\[
(\alpha-24,\alpha+3,\alpha-1/10,
199\alpha/200+200,199\alpha/200+1/25,2\alpha/5+1/25)
\]
form a counterexample. Their generator equals $M+(\alpha-25)(q\mathbf1^\top-QI)$. All nonzero eigenvalues shift by $-(\alpha-25)Q$, while their ordering, modes, overlaps and all $F,G$ remain unchanged. Positivity follows directly from $\alpha>24$.

\subsection{A predictive mechanism and an exact onset threshold}\label{sec:mechanism}
The following construction controls the eigenvector rather than interpreting it after diagonalization. It extends the rational-mode companion in Appendix B, not merely the reset direction: two rates and the slow eigenvector change with a parameter. Throughout the entire parameter interval, the four triplet predicates fail. Nevertheless, the family crosses a sharp boundary between ordinary and anomalous asymptotic cooling.

\begin{lemma}[A single-turn criterion for a nearly neutral top state]\label{lem:singleturn}
Let $E_1=0<E_2<E_3<E_4$, $\beta_b=1$, and suppose the simple slow left mode, normalized with $w_1=1$, is
\[
w=(1,-p,-r,z),\qquad p>r>-z>0,\qquad \sum_iw_i e^{-E_i}=0.
\]
Assume also
\[
E_4>E_2+E_3,\qquad (1+r)E_3>(p-r)(E_3-E_2).
\]
Write $E_\Sigma=E_2+E_3+E_4$ and
\[
D_0=(1-p-r+z)E_\Sigma+4(pE_2+rE_3-zE_4).
\]
There is an ordinary asymptotic cooling Mpemba effect at finite positive initial temperatures if and only if $D_0<0$. In that case $|b_2(\beta)|$ has exactly one maximum on $0<\beta<1$. If $D_0\geq0$, it strictly decreases throughout that interval. There is no asymptotic heating reversal for $\beta>1$ under these hypotheses.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $N(\beta)=\sum_iw_i e^{-\beta E_i}$ and $Z(\beta)=\sum_i e^{-\beta E_i}$, so $b_2=N/Z$. All three excited-state coefficients are negative, hence $N'(\beta)>0$. Since $N(1)=0$, $b_2<0$ for $0\leq\beta<1$ and $b_2>0$ for $\beta>1$.

Define $D(\beta)=Z(\beta)^2b_2'(\beta)$. Its pair expansion has three positive and three negative terms:
\begin{align*}
D(\beta)={}&c_{12}e^{-\beta E_2}+c_{13}e^{-\beta E_3}+c_{14}e^{-\beta E_4}\\
&-c_{23}e^{-\beta(E_2+E_3)}-c_{24}e^{-\beta(E_2+E_4)}-c_{34}e^{-\beta(E_3+E_4)},
\end{align*}
where $c_{1j}=(w_1-w_j)E_j>0$ and $c_{ij}=(w_j-w_i)(E_j-E_i)>0$ for $2\leq i<j\leq4$. In particular $c_{13}>c_{23}$. Multiplying by a positive exponential gives
\begin{align*}
J(\beta):=e^{\beta E_4}D(\beta)
={}&c_{12}e^{\beta(E_4-E_2)}
+e^{\beta(E_4-E_2-E_3)}(c_{13}e^{\beta E_2}-c_{23})\\
&+c_{14}-c_{24}e^{-\beta E_2}-c_{34}e^{-\beta E_3}.
\end{align*}
Every nonconstant summand is strictly increasing for $\beta\geq0$; the second is a product of positive increasing functions. Thus $J'>0$. Also $J(1)>0$ because $N'(1)>0$ and $N(1)=0$. Direct evaluation gives $J(0)=D_0$. If $D_0<0$, there is exactly one zero $\beta_m\in(0,1)$, with $b_2'<0$ before it and $b_2'>0$ afterwards. As $b_2<0$, this is exactly a maximum of its absolute value. Two finite $0<\beta_h<\beta_c<\beta_m$ give a strict cooling witness. If $D_0\geq0$, then $J(\beta)>0$ for every $\beta>0$, excluding any such reversal. Finally $J>0$ and $b_2>0$ for $\beta>1$, giving ordinary heating ordering.
\end{proof}
The condition uses $\beta=0$ only as an analytic endpoint. Infinite initial temperature is not required for any witness. The single-turn proof is essential: the sign of an endpoint derivative alone would not generally exclude a maximum elsewhere.

\paragraph{Rates that force this geometry while every triplet fails.}
Keep $q=(1,199/200,2/5,1/400)$ and set
\[
p=\frac{759}{1000},\quad r_0=\frac{48959}{80000},\quad
\eta=\frac1{160},\quad -\frac3{100}\leq z\leq-\frac1{250}.
\]
Use $w(z)=(1,-p,-r_0-\eta z,z)$. The small compensation $\eta z$ makes $q^\top w(z)=0$ exactly. Define the downward rates by
\[
a_{12}=1,\qquad a_{13}=28,\qquad a_{14}=249/10,\qquad a_{24}=4983/200,
\]
\begin{align*}
a_{23}(z)&=\frac{528238451563-2257386450z}{200000(11761-500z)},\\
a_{34}(z)&=\frac{2(1550z^2-5989657z+1197903)}{5(80500z+48959)}.
\end{align*}
As before, $a_{ji}=a_{ij}q_j/q_i$ and the diagonal enforces column conservation. These formulas arise by taking the first component of $Lw=\lambda w$ to define
\[
\lambda(z)=-\frac{1550z+3973343}{200000},
\]
then solving its second and fourth components for $a_{23}$ and $a_{34}$. The third follows from bath orthogonality and stationarity; direct substitution checks all four. This is a rate-level design prescription. At $z=-1/250$ it reproduces Appendix B exactly.

\begin{theorem}[A controlled triplet-failing family with a sharp cooling threshold]\label{thm:family}
Every generator just defined is strictly positive and reversible, fails all four original triplet tests strictly, and has the displayed $w(z)$ as its simple slow mode. Put
\begin{align*}
U&=(1-p-r_0)E_\Sigma+4(pE_2+r_0E_3),\\
V&=(3+\eta)E_4-(1-\eta)E_2-(1+3\eta)E_3>0,\qquad z_*=U/V.
\end{align*}
Within the stated closed parameter interval, an ordinary asymptotic cooling effect occurs exactly when
\[
\boxed{z>z_*,\qquad -0.017940112<z_*<-0.017940111.}
\]
For $z\leq z_*$ there is no asymptotic cooling effect. For $z>z_*$ the cooling amplitude has a unique maximum; the family has no asymptotic heating effect.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Both rate denominators stay positive. Exact polynomial bounds throughout the interval give $224<a_{23}<225$, $10<a_{34}<12$, and the following uniform bounds:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule Triplet&Upper bound on $F$&Upper bound on $G$\\\midrule
123&$-230$&$-196$\\
124&$-1/50$&$-13/100$\\
134&$-19/10$&$-2/25$\\
234&$-118$&$-3/100$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every comparison is strict; $123,124$ stay in the upper $\kappa$ branch and $134,234$ in the lower branch.

To identify the slow mode uniformly, let $M_0$ use the four fixed rates above and $a_{23}=224$, $a_{34}=10$. An exact $LDL^\top$ congruence of $\diag(q)(M_0^\top+50I)$ has successive pivots
\[
\frac{150971}{4000},\quad
-\frac{494381936093}{12077680000},\quad
\frac{2188881388080131}{19775277443720},\quad
-\frac{432951459178371}{17511051104641048}.
\]
There are two negative pivots, so $M_0$ has exactly two eigenvalues below $-50$. Increasing conductances increases the reversible Dirichlet form. The min--max principle therefore puts two eigenvalues of every $M(z)$ below $-50$ as well. The explicit eigenvalue satisfies $-20<\lambda(z)<-19$; the remaining eigenvalue is the simple stationary zero. Consequently $\lambda(z)$ is the simple slow nonzero eigenvalue, with separation exceeding 30. This rules out realizing the desired geometry in a faster mode by mistake.

The hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:singleturn} hold uniformly, and substitution of $r=r_0+\eta z$ gives $D_0=U-Vz$. The exact logarithm enclosures described below prove $V>0$ and the displayed bracket for $z_*$. Apply the lemma, including its equality case.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Predictions for finite preparations and for onset.}
The pair $\beta_h=1/1000$, $\beta_c=1/100$ has its own exact threshold. Write
\[
u(\beta)=\frac{1-pq_2^\beta-r_0q_3^\beta}{Z(\beta)},\qquad
v(\beta)=\frac{q_4^\beta-\eta q_3^\beta}{Z(\beta)},\qquad b_z=u+zv.
\]
Since both overlaps are negative, this pair reverses if and only if
\[
z>z_{\rm pair}:=-\frac{u(1/1000)-u(1/100)}{v(1/1000)-v(1/100)}
\simeq-0.01480024798314.
\]
The denominator is certified positive by integer-power enclosures. For $z_*<z\leq z_{\rm pair}$ other, sufficiently high but finite temperatures witness the effect; this particular pair does not. This distinguishes existence of the effect from success of a chosen temperature grid.

Let $d_*=\partial_\beta D(0,z_*)>0$ and $\delta=z-z_*$. Analyticity and the implicit function theorem give, as $\delta\downarrow0$,
\begin{align*}
\beta_m(z)&=\frac{V}{d_*}\delta+O(\delta^2),\\
|b_z(\beta_m)|-|b_z(0)|&=\frac{V^2}{32d_*}\delta^2+O(\delta^3).
\end{align*}
Here $V/d_*\simeq1.76871$ and $V^2/(32d_*)\simeq0.943684$ in the fixed normalization $w_1=1$. The factors follow from $Z(0)=4$, $b_z'(0)=-V\delta/16$ and $b_{z_*}''(0)=d_*/16$. The peak is born at the high-temperature endpoint with a linearly opening inverse-temperature window and a quadratically small advantage. Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism} illustrates this prediction.

\caption{Predictive family of Theorem~\ref{thm:family}. All four triplet predicates fail throughout. Above $z_*$ the amplitude initially increases with inverse temperature and has exactly one cooling maximum; below $z_*$ it decreases throughout cooling. The shaded region has $\partial_\beta|b_z|>0$, so any two finite inverse temperatures inside one vertical slice give a witness. The $\beta=0$ limit is only a reference for the left ordinate. Curves are numerical; the onset and single-turn classification have a continuous-parameter proof.}
\label{fig:mechanism}
\end{figure}


This makes the neutral-state explanation predictive. Raising $z$ toward zero decreases the negative slow-mode weight carried by state 4, while its small bath weight requires only the weak adjustment $\eta z$ in state 3. The two rate formulas enforce that geometry, the spectral comparison makes it dynamically dominant, and $U-Vz<0$ quantifies when high-temperature occupation of state 4 reverses thermal ordering. The theorem classifies this controlled family and the stated single-turn geometry; it does not classify every four-state counterexample or every finite-time transient crossing. The covariance and spectral tools are established; no exhaustive priority claim is made for this particular construction or lemma.

\paragraph{Continuous-parameter verification.}
Run \path{research/mechanism_certificate.py}. It checks the rational eigenvector identities, all original triplet inequalities and the two branches on the entire interval. For each rational inequality, it clears the nonvanishing denominator, substitutes $z=-3/100+(13/500)t$, and verifies positive Bernstein coefficients on $0\leq t\leq1$. Since the Bernstein basis is nonnegative and sums to one, these are certificates over a continuum, not sampled evidence. The script records every coefficient and the baseline inertia pivots; a second spectral check bounds the remaining quadratic using its value at $-50$ and the mean of its two real roots.

Logarithms are reduced to arguments in $[1,2]$. With $x=(a-1)/(a+1)$, the positive series $2\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}x^{2k+1}/(2k+1)$ bounds $\log a$ from below, and its remainder is at most $2x^{2m+1}/[(2m+1)(1-x^2)]$. Rational bounds with $m=45$ certify the onset bracket and coefficient signs. Integer bisection bounds the algebraic Gibbs weights for the finite-pair threshold. Separately, \path{research/mechanism_plots.py} checks eight family members against symmetric diagonalization and numerical differentiation at 65-digit precision. Those checks and the plotted curves are illustrative cross-checks, not the continuous proof.


\subsection{Visible reversal and its size}
Figures~\ref{fig:overlap} and \ref{fig:crossing} separate two facts: the hotter initial state has a smaller slow-mode amplitude, and the full time-dependent distance actually changes ordering. These use the primary generator in Theorem~1, with the bath and generator fixed throughout. The high-temperature amplitude reaches its numerical maximum near $\beta=0.0237422$.

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Thermal overlap of the exact primary generator, with $w_1=1$ and $T_b=1$. The broad curve conceals a small high-temperature maximum; the enlargement displays it. The blue preparation has a larger slow amplitude than the hotter red preparation. The enlarged ordinate uses the hot amplitude as its reference; the quoted 0.1102\% reduction uses the cold amplitude. Numerical curves use 80-digit arithmetic; the two selected overlaps are independently certified exactly.}
\label{fig:overlap}
\end{figure}


Initially $D_h(0)\simeq1.210137886$ and $D_c(0)\simeq1.155489557$, so the hotter preparation is farther away in relative entropy. The two distances cross numerically near $t=0.14967233$. Exact rational bounds prove a crossing in $(0.14,0.16)$: at the endpoints,
\begin{align*}
D_h(0.14)-D_c(0.14)&\in(5.9361291891,5.9361291893)\,10^{-8},\\
D_h(0.16)-D_c(0.16)&\in(-1.3100324358,-1.3100324356)\,10^{-8}.
\end{align*}
Total variation also reverses, with a certified crossing in $(0.10,0.11)$ and numerical location $0.10436410$. The difference between these times is expected: finite-time crossings depend on the distance. Continuity plus the certified signs proves existence; no uniqueness or earliest-crossing theorem is asserted.

The relevant fractional amplitude reduction is
\[
\epsilon_b=1-\frac{|b_h|}{|b_c|}\simeq0.0011018531=0.11018531\%.
\]
For relative entropy the leading term is quadratic, so
\[
1-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{D_h(t)}{D_c(t)}
=1-\frac{b_h^2}{b_c^2}\simeq0.22024921\%.
\]
Both preparations have the same decay exponent $\lambda_2$. This is an ordinary effect, not the exponentially faster strong effect. For an increasingly small common distance threshold, the asymptotic time advance is
\[
\Delta t=\frac{\log(|b_c|/|b_h|)}{|\lambda_2|}
\simeq5.5488\,10^{-5}\,a_{12}^{-1}.
\]
The slow relaxation time is $1/|\lambda_2|\simeq0.0503313\,a_{12}^{-1}$, so this advance is only about $0.11025\%$ of one slow relaxation time. It is not a percentage reduction in the full cooling time, which depends on the chosen threshold. The time unit has no assigned experimental value, and $T_h/T_b=1000$, $T_c/T_b=100$. These data establish a strict mathematical failure of the triplet inference; they do not establish a practically useful cooling protocol.

\subsection{Triplet-rate applications of stochastic monotonicity}
Let $L=M^\top$ act on functions, $(Lf)_i=\sum_{j\ne i}a_{ji}(f_j-f_i)$. Define adjacent differences $y_i=f_{i+1}-f_i$, the difference map $Bf=y$, and $(H_0y)_j=\sum_{\ell<j}y_\ell$. Then $BL=KB$ with $K=BLH_0$, since $L\mathbf1=0$. In the constant-plus-differences basis, $L$ is block triangular with blocks 0 and $K$, so $K$ has precisely the nonzero generator eigenvalues. For its off-diagonal entries,
\[
K_{i\ell}=\begin{cases}
\sum_{j\leq\ell}(a_{j,i}-a_{j,i+1}),&\ell<i,\\
\sum_{j>\ell}(a_{j,i+1}-a_{j,i}),&\ell>i.
\end{cases}
\]
\begin{theorem}[Application of stochastic monotonicity]
For any finite number of states, strictly ordered energies, a fixed $0<\beta_b<\infty$, positive reversible rates and a simple slowest nonzero eigenvalue, if every off-diagonal entry of $K$ is nonnegative, there is no asymptotic cooling or heating Mpemba effect at positive initial temperatures.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Such a matrix $K$ is called Metzler. After a sufficiently large scalar diagonal shift it is nonnegative. Perron theory gives a nonzero nonnegative eigenvector $y$ at its spectral abscissa, which is $\lambda_2$. This does not require irreducibility of $K$. Choose $w$ with $Bw=y$ and $\pi_b^\top w=0$. Then $B(Lw-\lambda_2w)=0$, so $Lw-\lambda_2w$ is constant. Its bath expectation is zero, hence it vanishes. Thus $w$ is the slow left eigenfunction, nondecreasing and nonconstant in energy.

The covariance identity now gives $b_2'(\beta)<0$ for every finite positive $\beta$: all its pairwise summands are nonnegative and at least one is strictly positive. Because $b_2(\beta_b)=0$, its absolute value decreases toward the bath on the cooling side and increases away from the bath on the heating side. These are exactly the normal asymptotic orderings.
\end{proof}
In four states, the six off-diagonal entries are
\begin{align*}
K_{12}&=a_{32}+a_{42}-a_{31}-a_{41},&K_{13}&=a_{42}-a_{41},\\
K_{21}&=a_{12}-a_{13},&K_{23}&=a_{43}-a_{42},\\
K_{31}&=a_{13}-a_{14},&K_{32}&=a_{13}+a_{23}-a_{14}-a_{24}.
\end{align*}
The original failures already ensure the three upper entries are nonnegative, since $G\leq0$ orders upward rates. A corrected, directly checkable sufficient condition is therefore the four original failures together with
\[
